On the surface a reviewer's policy reads, a planted approval is a pending F like any other; on the public record its review is shaped as a real review under an alias of the known class, and `audit_odds_at_verdict` shows a value drawn once at the verdict from the trailing real approvals' recorded odds until the reveal (§14).

**Rule — the plant rate.** In each period a fraction $\pi_t$ of real initial reviews are plants — one plant per $1/\pi_t$ real initial assignments — with bad plants a fraction $\beta_P$ of them. $\pi_t$ is set by the effort dial (§12.2) and is **public**; which cases are plants is not.

**Consequence.** The **bad-plant approval rate** $\hat F_t$ — the share of bad plants approved in period $t$ — is a measurement of the reserve's negligence that no panel produces and no majority can paint. It is the clock the rest of this baseline runs on.

**Limit.** A plant must be constructible: a submission whose fulfillment the orchestrator knows and a reviewer cannot tell from real work. That is easy where fulfillment is mechanical (tests, hashes, reproducible outputs) and hard where it is semantic. The mechanism's reach is therefore domain-dependent; this is a limit on where the loop can be deployed first, not on the loop (§18.1 question 10). In a domain where it does not hold, the loop is in 1.1.0's position: fair with both challengers present, blind under capture.

### 7.7 Troops — the orchestrator's own reviewers and deliverers *(1.4.0)*

**Rule.** A **troop** is a reviewer or deliverer owned by the orchestrator, summoned for **one seat** and released at that seat's completion. A troop

- is **not a member**: it holds no wallet of its own — **the duty fund is its wallet** (§12.3); it has no $\rho$, no $\kappa$, no membership state, no plant record, and no capacity of its own; it is never on duty in the reserve, never counts toward $E_t$, the duty cap or the panel floor, and is never selected — it is **summoned** (§12.3);
- is **honest by construction** and **fallible by declaration**: a troop reviewer's verdict is the truth with probability $1-\varepsilon_T$, $\varepsilon_T$ a declared default (careful's 0.05) — the orchestrator's own disposition, supplied by the driver through the ordinary review and submission operations as every member's verdict is; its verdict may be appealed by the actor and challenged by the requester exactly as any verdict, and when it is overturned the fund pays the court fee and the rest of the bond burns, as for any reviewer;
- **accepts every verdict on its own work, at the verdict**: a troop never appeals, never disputes, never revises, never delegates, never posts a nested request; a rejection, a $\mathsf P$ or a $\bot$ on a troop's delivery is final $\mathsf N$ the moment it is given, the case settles and the troop is released — the response window exists for a party that might appeal, and a troop has nothing to decide;
- is paid as a reviewer is paid and bears what a reviewer bears, with the fund as its wallet: at summons the fund **purchases the troop's declared cost** ($c_T$ for a review, $c^D_T$ for a delivery) as a resource purchase (§4.4: value-neutral; not a bond burn; I3's two burns stand) and posts the bond $G^V$ or the guarantee $G$ from the fund; at completion its expenses are reimbursed from the allowance $\varphi^E$ up to $K$ as any reviewer's; at settlement the fee, the price, a court fee won, a released bond or guarantee go **to the fund**, and a forfeited guarantee or burned bond leave it;
- **claims no emission and no uplift**: a troop's role weight is not minted (as unclaimed emission is not) and is excluded from the batch's uplift distribution;
- **never reviews a plant and never acts in one**: the orchestrator does not test itself — until 2.8.0 a plant whose seat found no market reviewer waited; **since 2.8.0 the orchestrator's own verdict takes a plant's seat past $w_R$** at the troop's step, the truth with probability $1-\varepsilon_T$, no fee, no loan, no place in $N_T$, counted apart (§7.6);
- is **recorded as a troop** in the History — troop id, seat kind, case, tick of summons, tick of release. The reviewer's view (§14) and the actor's view are unchanged: a party sees of a troop what it sees of any reviewer.

**Consequence.** A troop adds a trust assumption only about ELEF's own servers, and ELEF is already the party trusted to plant honestly, keep the chain and rule on splits. It adds nothing a member could envy: no awards competed for, no reputation, no emission, no uplift, no seat in the reserve.

**Recorded and rejected.** A *member seat held for probationers' audits* (spends delivery capacity on standby, whether or not an audit comes); *probation paused when no member is free to audit* (stalls first looks for a shortage not of the probationer's making); a *provisional approval* on its own (right in what it refuses — adopted as the obligation of §8.3 — but converting the shortage into waiting rather than supplying what is short); the *duty levy* of 1.3.0 stays rejected; *pricing a troop's delivery at the customer's ceiling* (rewards the state for scarcity, §12.3).

**Rule (2.0.0; the burn amended 2.1.0) — the state at its expected cost, its price unchanged; the capacity parameter.** A troop delivers at $\bar B$ (§12.3: the trailing mean of members' awarded prices, only where $\beta\ge\bar B$, after the members have taken theirs) — *a request past $w_A$ goes to the state at $\bar B$, not at its cost*, so the state never undercuts a member (run 13). **The burn (2.1.0)** is the state's **expected cost**, $c^D_T=\max\!\big(0,\ (1-b_T)\,\bar B-b_T\,G\big)$, with $G=\lceil0.5\,\bar B\rceil$ the delivery's guarantee and $b_T$ the **trailing fail rate of the state's deliveries**: the share ending in a final $\mathsf N$ among the state's deliveries closed in the last closed period, if it closed ten or more; else over the trailing closed periods extended back until ten; else the prior $b=0.2$ (run 12's base). The burn is at the summons. A delivery that vests returns $\bar B$ and its locks; one that ends in a final $\mathsf N$ returns nothing and forfeits $G$. **In expectation the delivery is neutral to $\Omega$:** a success leaves $\bar B-c^D_T=b_T(\bar B+G)$, a failure costs $c^D_T+G=(1-b_T)(\bar B+G)$, and at the trailing rate they cancel — neither margin nor bleed. **The charge is automatic, up to two thirds:** if the state's deliveries fail more often, $b_T$ rises, the burn falls, and the state keeps more of each success until its losses are covered; with $G=\lceil0.5\bar B\rceil$ the burn is $\bar B(1-1.5\,b_T)$ — zero at $b_T=2/3$, and above that no burn makes the state neutral (a success returns $\bar B$, a failure forfeits $G$, and $(1-b)\bar B<bG$): there the automatic charge runs out and only §10.4's wall is left. There is no other charge: the state's price does not move, and neither does ELEF's fee. *The 2.0.0 default $c^D_T=\bar B$ — "at cost", which run 12 showed to be below cost, a fifth of the state's deliveries failing and losing $\bar B+G$ with nothing to offset it — is the control; the 1.x $c^D_T=10$ at $P_0$, a margin of half of $\bar B$, the other.* A troop's review-seat cost $c_T$ stays indexed and its fee is $\Omega$'s. A troop's cost in credits is a **policy number** that prices the state's work against the members' for the market's sake, not a server cost: the state's physical capacity is **$N_T$, the most troops ELEF can field at once** — deliveries and review seats together — a parameter outside the ledger (§12.3).

---

## 8. Challenges: appeal, dispute, audit, ruling

**Rule.** One challenge round per case. A dispute consumes it; an audit that disagrees consumes it; an audit that agrees does not. There is no appeal against appeal reviewers.

**Rule.** The challenger pays the court fee $D$ to $\Omega$ at filing. If the challenger wins, the liable bond reimburses $D$ to the challenger; if it loses, nothing further moves. The loser bears the fee. (2.8.0: on a plant the fee is the bank's, destroyed at filing, and no bond pays it back — §7.6.) The grounds of a challenge are text and are committed to the record with the challenge; they are shown to the reviewers who decide it.

**Rule — the planted lie (2.9.0).** Beside the plant's truthful challenges (2.8.0), the orchestrator files planted lies: the plant actor appeals a bad plant's N and the plant requester disputes a good plant's F, at rates the orchestrator sets — $\pi^{La}_t$ per real appeal and $\pi^{Ld}_t$ per real dispute of the trailing closed period, with a floor of two a period of each kind, so a panel kind no real challenger uses is still tested (where none does, the floor makes that panel a seat only plants reach, read as the base rate it is). A planted lie takes the real challenge's path — filed in the pass at a drawn position, at a delay drawn from the trailing real challenges' window of the kind, truthful and lying alike (the stated default where empty), its grounds the population's own, the court fee minted to the alias and destroyed at filing, two fresh reviewers — and the panel's verdicts are adjudicated by truth: a panelist that sides with the lie (F on the bad plant's appeal, N on the good plant's dispute) is a plant failure, one that holds the truth stands; the case resolves by truth with no second overturn and no ruling. The first reviewer, whose verdict the lie contests, is not adjudicated again. A lying appeal spends the bad plant's N — the plant actor's acceptance under 2.8.0 — for a panel; that is its cost. **The rate's spending (the eighth conformance item of the instrument, Appendix S, after run 39):** a coin at $\pi^{L}_t$ per real challenge of the kind filed in the pass, the lie filed in the same pass on a plant of the kind at hand (else on the next), and the floor of two filed at ticks drawn from the trailing real challenges' positions in their periods (uniform over the period where the window is empty) — so the planted lies take the real challenges' positions in the period; the trailing period's count times the rate is the expected count, kept as the record.

### 8.1 Appeal by the actor

After $\mathsf N$ or $\bot$ the actor pays $D$, states its grounds $d_q$, and two fresh reviewers receive the submission, the appealed assessment and the grounds. Matching verdicts settle the appeal; a split goes to the orchestrator.

**An appeal of $\mathsf N$:** overturned → $G$ released at vesting, $D$ reimbursed from the original reviewer's bond, that reviewer's remaining bond burned, its reliability down and its audit rate doubled; upheld → $G$ forfeited, $D$ borne by the actor.

**An appeal of $\bot$ answers two questions**, both stated explicitly by each appeal reviewer and never inferred one from the other:

1. **Objection** — was the first reviewer right that the evidence was missing? `upheld` or `rejected`.
2. **Fulfillment** — $\mathsf F$ or $\mathsf N$. An upheld objection entails $\mathsf N$: what cannot be assessed cannot be fulfilled.

| Both reviewers say | The $\bot$ | Court fee | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| rejected, same verdict | overturned | reimbursed to the actor | their verdict |
| rejected, different verdicts | overturned | reimbursed | the orchestrator rules on fulfillment |
| upheld | stands; its reviewer is paid as correct | borne by the actor | $\mathsf N$ — an unverifiable submission is the actor's failure |
| split on the objection | — | — | the orchestrator rules on **both** questions |

An appeal reviewer is overruled if its objection disposition differs from the settled one, or — when the objection is rejected — if its verdict differs from the settled outcome.

### 8.2 Dispute by the requester

Within the window, the requester of a root case may dispute a pending $\mathsf F$ on the same terms: fee, grounds, two fresh reviewers, orchestrator on a split. Upheld → the approving reviewer's bond pays the fee and the rest burns; final $\mathsf N$ for the actor. Rejected → the requester bears the fee; the case vests. A dispute is admitted only if the round is free, the window is open, the grounds are text, and the requester can fund the fee — all checked before anything changes. A requester may not dispute a case a human has ruled on (§15).

### 8.3 Audit of approvals

**Rule.** The orchestrator assigns blind audits of pending $\mathsf F$ verdicts, each to a fresh reviewer that receives the submission and not the case's verdicts, challenge records or reviewer identities. The orchestrator assigns audits; it does not perform them, because it also rules. The verdict is committed to the record before the audit is assigned. Opening an audit suspends vesting.

**Rule (1.4.0) — a rolled audit is an obligation of the case.** The orchestrator rolls the audit on the applied odds — **since 2.8.0, the odds the approving reviewer carried at the verdict, recorded on every case at its initial verdict and read by the roll** (one number, one path, so a plant is audited at the rate its reviewer's real cases are: 1.2.0's adjudication doubles a failed reviewer's odds before the roll; a real case's live odds differed from its verdict's on half a percent of the rolls, a reviewer overturned on another case in the same tick — Appendix U) — and **records the roll**; from the roll the case's window does not close and the case does not vest until the audit completes. It is seated by a panel-eligible on-duty member when one is free, as before; after $w_R$ ticks unfilled, by a troop (§12.3); the audit of a troop reviewer's approval of a troop's delivery carries odds 1 and is **a member's only** — no troop audits the state's own work — so it is the one audit seat that may wait past its window, held and counted (§12.3). There is no exception to the obligation: nothing a probationer approved vests unaudited — by a member, by a troop, or not at all; a seat the fund cannot carry stays unfilled, the summons is retried at every tick's seating, and the case holds (the actor's capacity was released at the verdict; its guarantee and the requester's price and pots stay in escrow until the audit completes — the honest outcome, counted as troop shortfall). A human ruling supersedes the obligation as it voids an open audit (I18). This is also a conformance correction of the drivers: since run 1 they had dropped a roll that found no auditor, because the engine offered no way to hold a case for one.

An audit is answered $\mathsf F$ or $\mathsf N$ (§3.3). Its agreement is judged against the case's **live** assessment. Agreement → a second settled review. Disagreement → the audit verdict stands as one appeal review, one more fresh reviewer is assigned, and the orchestrator rules on a split, with the consequences of §8.1. If a requester disputes while an audit is open or after it agreed, the audit verdict is one of the two dispute reviews. No audit is assigned to a case a human has ruled on; an audit open at a ruling is voided. **Since 2.8.0 a plant's pending $\mathsf F$ is audited as any pending $\mathsf F$** and the auditor's verdict is adjudicated by the plant's truth at once (§7.6).

**Rule — the planted approval's audit (2.9.0).** A planted approval's pending F is audited with certainty: the case carries its own mark, its live audit odds are 1 and the obligation is recorded at the verdict (as I25 records a troop's own delivery's obligation, and nothing more of that path — the seat is not `market_audit_only`), counted in the period's approvals and the audit demand at 1. The audit seat is **a member's or nobody's** (the ruling after run 38, Appendix V): a free eligible member fills it at the step a real audit seat is filled; the orchestrator's own verdict never takes it — a confirmation by the state would be the state's judgment, not the market's — so past $w_R$ the pass passes it by (what it would have done is read), and a planted approval no member has audited by its window's close **closes by truth at the window's end, spent and read** (not adjudicated, not in $\hat F^A$); the auditor's verdict is adjudicated by the plant's truth (a confirmation a plant failure, a rejection stood, a rejection opening the third seat as on a real case). The plant requester disputes the planted approval at the real requesters' rate, as any bad plant's F: where the real requesters dispute first, the planted approval meets the dispute panel and not the audit, and **a dispute's settlement discharges a standing audit obligation** (2.9.0 §4, every case — a case whose rolled audit still stands when its dispute is settled has had its approval judged by two fresh reviewers; while the dispute is open the standing obligation is not a seat required). **The state's own work** (1.4.0 §2.2, I25; the second reading in Appendix V, ruled after run 39): the dispute's panel is the market's judgment where a member sat on it and discharges the obligation as a member's audit would; where the panel was the state's alone the audit seat is admitted after the dispute — the one challenge round yields to I25's purpose, the round existing to limit a participant's exposure and the state being none (one review position beyond $V_{\max}$); a case is never left standing.

**Rule (1.3.0) — pinned odds for the unproven.** A **newcomer's** audit odds are pinned at $1$ until its bad-plant record is determined ($n_{\min}$ bad plants adjudicated, §11.2): every approval is audited. So are a **probationer's**, for as long as it is one. The §8.3 update below does not run while the odds are pinned. (The 1.2.0 taper — odds of 1 until five approvals in a row have stood — was easy to pass by stamping: about 80% of work is good, so a third of stampers passed it at once.) The cost — about fifty fully audited reviews per newcomer — has a known worst case: in a fresh population every actor is undetermined, every approval is audited by an auditor that is itself an undetermined newcomer, and review demand doubles in the first period.

**Default (adaptive audit rate).** Once determined, $p_j$ follows from $p_{\text{start}}=1.0$: halves after every 20 consecutive verdicts that stood, floor $0.5$ (the economy reviewer's defaults move of November 6, 2026, under §19 — Appendix A, Appendix V; 5 and 0.2 from 1.2.0 until then); doubles on any overturn or plant failure, cap $1.0$. An actor readmitted from probation restarts at $p_{\text{start}}$. The odds are **applied** as $\min(1,\ m_t\,p_j)$ — the dial of §12.2 scales them — and as $1$ while pinned. **Default (1.4.0, chosen at implementation; Appendix I):** a troop reviewer has no $p_j$; its approval of a member's work carries the adaptive floor scaled by the dial, $\min(1,\ m_t\,p_{\text{floor}})$ — the odds a reviewer whose record stood would carry; its approval of a troop's delivery carries $1$ (§12.3).

### 8.4 The orchestrator's ruling

$Z_q=\operatorname{Adjudicate}_O(\mathcal E_D)$ over the full record of the challenge. The ruling is the accepted result for settlement, not a claim of objective truth. The known incentive to predict the orchestrator rather than the truth is addressed by human review of rulings (§15), not assumed away.

---

## 9. Delegation

### 9.1 A nested request is a full loop

**Rule.** While committed to $q$, actor $A$ may post a nested request $q'$ with $\operatorname{root}(q')=\operatorname{root}(q)$ and a maximum price $\beta'$. Posting locks the nested **all-in** — $\beta'+\varphi^{E\prime}+\varphi^{F\prime}+\omega_0 m_t\beta'$ *(2.3.0: the parent posts the pots, §4.3, I36; 1.1.0–2.2.0: $\beta'$ alone)* — and $G^V$ from $A$'s unlocked balance and freezes $q'$'s nominals at that index (§4.5). On award, $B'$ moves into escrow, the pots and the fee are drawn from the lock and the rest is released; the $G^V$ stays locked as $A$'s **rejection bond**. **A probationer wins no award of any kind** — a nested request refuses one as a root does (2.3.0 §2.1, I33). The winner $B$ locks its own guarantee. $A$ is $q'$'s requester: it judges $B$'s result, and its judgment is $q'$'s initial verdict.

**Rule.** $A$ may submit $q$ only after every nested request it posted has reached its **milestone**: its initial verdict, or a terminal state — withdrawn, cancelled, or final $\mathsf N$ without a submission (a missed delivery). Nested vesting is independent of the parent's; $A$ carries that risk.

**Consequence.** The root pointer forbids rootless requests, not unnecessary ones. What constrains unnecessary delegation is that $A$ pays for every nested award — and, since 2.3.0, the child's pots and fee — from its own margin; the gain from trade is $B-B'-$ the overhead (the nested fee, the pots' net cost, the parent's care in judging), positive for a dear parent and a cheap child. **A broker** — a parent that delegates most of what it wins — is a structure the rules permit; its accountability under §9.2, not its existence, is the reading. **Rule (2.3.0) — the troops never *deliver* a nested request.** The per-request summons (§12.3) reads root requests only for deliveries — as the engine has since 1.4.0; a nested request past $w_A$ is the parent's problem, as §9.3 has it. Review seats on nested cases stay the troops' when the market cannot seat them. **A nested request has no service clock** (§3.1): the clock and the rebate are the root's.

### 9.2 Accountability cascades; one guarantee secures one obligation

**Consequence.** $B$'s guarantee is to $A$: on $B$'s final nonfulfillment it forfeits to $A$. $A$'s guarantee is to its own requester. If $A$ delivers bad work built on $B$'s bad work and did not catch it, $A$ forfeits upstream with no recourse. If $A$ catches it, $A$ rejects, $B$ forfeits to $A$, and $B$ may appeal exactly as under §8.1 — which keeps $A$ from rejecting good work to collect guarantees.

**Rule.** The rejection bond is released the moment $A$ accepts; acceptance is not bonded, because its downside flows through $A$'s own delivery. If $A$ rejects, the bond is $A$'s reviewer bond for $B$'s appeal: on overturn it pays $B$'s court fee and the remainder burns; if the rejection stands, it is released at settlement. The principle: bond the verdict whose wrongness harms the *other* party with no other recourse. A wrong acceptance harms $A$; a wrong rejection harms $B$. $A$'s guarantee secures one obligation — delivery of $q$ — and nothing else; a nested acceptance overturned by audit forfeits $B$'s guarantee to $A$ and lowers $A$'s reliability, with no separate monetary loss to $A$.

### 9.3 Exits: self-execution, cancellation, pool exhaustion

**Rule.** Self-execution is always an exit: $A$ may do the nested work itself at its own cost whenever that is possible. Before award, $A$ withdraws $q'$ freely. After award and **before the earlier of $B$'s submission and $q'$'s deadline** (2.5.0; "before $B$ submits" alone until it), $A$ may cancel by paying $B$ a **kill fee** of $\min(C_B,K)$ — $B$'s execution cost on $q'$ as §4.6 defines it, the credits it burned on the case, so zero for a child that never worked — from the nested escrow first, then $A$'s unlocked balance, and only if $A$ can fund it; $B$'s guarantee and $A$'s rejection bond are released, $B$ is unmarked, and the fee counts in $A$'s cost. Once $B$ has submitted, $A$ must judge. **Rule (2.5.0) — no cancellation at or after the deadline.** At tick $d$, $q'$'s deadline, the window is the child's — it may still submit on its last tick (the deadline is inclusive, §6.1) — and there is nothing for the parent to cancel: a cancellation attempted at or after the deadline is **refused with no effect**; a parent that wants out acts by $d-1$. **The lapse, as it stands (stated, not new).** A nested request awarded to $B$ whose deadline arrives without a submission is final $\mathsf N$ at that tick, after the tick's submissions have been read — a missed delivery, §9.1's terminal state: $B$'s guarantee forfeits to $A$ (§9.2), $A$'s rejection bond is released, $q'$'s pots settle at its closure (2.3.0), the milestone §9.1 requires is reached and $A$ may submit from the next step. Two paths from one fact — no submission by the deadline — now end in one place for the child; a parent that cancels early, because it no longer needs the work or will not wait, still pays the fee and releases the guarantee: the parent's choice and the child's protection. The lowballer's miss costs it $G=\lceil 0.5B'\rceil$.

**Rule.** If $q'$ has been open and unawarded for $T_{\mathrm{wait}}$ ticks, $A$ cannot self-execute, and the parent's own deadline has not yet arrived, $A$ may withdraw its commitment to $q$ as *pool-exhaustion nonfulfillment*: $\rho_{\mathrm{pool}}\,G$ is forfeited and the rest released; it is not actor fault. If the deadline arrives first it is an ordinary miss. When the free pool is thin the orchestrator awards nested requests before root requests, since finishing a tree frees actors and starting a root consumes one.

**Rule.** On every exit — final $\mathsf N$ for any reason, pool exhaustion, cancellation — the exiting case's unawarded nested requests are withdrawn with it and the locks behind them released; awarded ones continue.

**Consequence.** A delegation tree commits as many actors as it has in-flight nodes, not its depth. The reserve (§12) guards verification capacity; these exits guard delegation capacity.

**Default.** $T_{\mathrm{wait}}=5$; $\rho_{\mathrm{pool}}=0.25$; "thin" is at most 2 free ordinary-eligible actors.

---

## 10. Settlement and accounting

### 10.1 Wallets and locks

$W_i=U_i+L_i$. Locking — a guarantee, a bond, a nested price ceiling, a rejection bond — moves $U\to L$ and leaves $W$ unchanged; release reverses it; forfeiture reduces $W$; expenses reduce $U$ and $W$; receipts increase both. **Every locked credit is attributable to a named obligation**, and a wallet's $L$ is the sum of its lock records. No double-pledging; no overdraft.

### 10.2 The actor

Fulfilled and vested: $\Delta W^{\mathrm{task}}_{iq}=B_{iq}-C_{iq}$. In general, with $P^{\mathrm{fail}}=0$:

$$\Delta W^{\mathrm{case}}_{iq}=P_{iq}-C_{iq}-I_D D_q+I_W D_q-I_N G_{iq}-I_{\mathrm{pool}}\,\rho_{\mathrm{pool}}G_{iq},\qquad I_N+I_{\mathrm{pool}}\le 1 .$$

### 10.3 The reviewer

$$\Delta W^V_{jv}=I_{\mathrm{paid}}\min(\text{spent},K)-\text{spent}+I_{\mathrm{stood}}\,\big(s_q\,f_{r(v)}+e_v\big)-I_{\mathrm{over}}\,G^V-I_{\mathrm{late}}\,\tfrac12 G^V ,$$

with $I_{\mathrm{paid}}=0$ for a withdrawn review, the court fee inside $I_{\mathrm{over}}G^V$ and owed only by the reviewer whose verdict was challenged, $f_{r(v)}$ the fixed fee by role and $s_q=\min(1,\varphi^F_{\text{left}}/\mathrm{Owed}_q)$ the fee-pot scale of §4.3.

### 10.4 Escrow and the operating account

**Rule (2.1.0) — the bank: a second account, three jobs, no discretion.** The orchestrator keeps two accounts in the ledger. **$\Omega$** is the state: the base fee and the troops' review fees in; rebates, the troops' costs and locks out. **$\mathfrak B$ is the bank**: the only account that creates and destroys credits. It has no wallet in the economy's sense — its balance is a *position*, the credits it has created and not yet destroyed — and it is not an actor: no bid, no verdict, no strategy, no interest. Everything it does is a rule, and everything it does is public. **Three jobs:** (i) it **mints by rule** — the requesters' issuance at the tick's start (§4.1), emission (§4.2), a plant's pots at its posting (§7.6), and a run's declared deficit shock (`mint_scenario`, by its note); (ii) it **publishes the index** once a period (§4.4); (iii) it **lends to the state against capital in flight** (below). It does nothing else: no payment to a member, a requester or $\Omega$ that is not one of the three. **Consequence.** "The state never printed for itself" is a sentence the ledger can prove: every credit the bank created is on one of three lines — minted, lent and repaid, or destroyed — and the state's own losses are on none of them (I28, I30).

**Rule (2.1.0) — the loan.** When a summons the rules call for — a delivery's $c^D_T$ and $G$, an inspection seat's $c_T$ and $G^V$ — exceeds $\Omega$'s unlocked balance, **the bank lends $\Omega$ the shortfall** (`loan`, a source), **tied to that summons**. The loan is capital in flight: it is repaid from **its own summons's return** — a delivery's at its vesting or final $\mathsf N$ (the price received, the guarantee released), a seat's at its review's settlement (the cost reimbursed, the fee, the bond released), which may fall before or after the case's close — before anything of that return reaches $\Omega$ (`repayment`, a sink). What the return does not cover is **the state's debt**; what exceeds the loan is $\Omega$'s income.

**Rule (2.1.0) — the debt, and the order of every inflow (ruled at acceptance, Appendix N).** Debt is the sum of every settled summons's unrepaid loan, and nothing else: the bank never lends for a loss, a rebate, or any payment that is not a summons. **Every inflow to $\Omega$'s unlocked balance is ordered the moment it arrives** — the base fee, the review fees, the surplus of a vesting delivery, inside the summons pass included: **debt → liabilities, oldest first → $\Omega$'s balance**. While the state owes the bank, no income of the state funds a troop — not a whole one, not part of one. The pass-start and tick-end points stand as I30's checks, not as the rule. $\Omega$'s balance is ELEF's retained income, and it funds the next summons before the bank does.

**Rule (2.2.0) — the rebate from income: the pool, the close, the rest forgone.** *The claim* is as §3.1 has it — recorded at the stop, the measure of lateness; a request withdrawn before its award is owed nothing. *The pool.* $\Omega$'s **fee income** — the base fee $\omega_0 B$ at an award or a summons, the troops' review fees at settlement, and a court fee a troop wins (income of the same kind: the troop's bond is the state's) — accrues during a period, **after the debt**, to the **period's pool**: **fee income only.** The returns of capital — a troop delivery's price and released guarantee at vesting, a seat's reimbursed cost and released bond at settlement — repay their own loans and land in $\Omega$'s balance as 2.1.0 has it, not in the pool: they are the state's working capital. Nor does a delivery's surplus $b_T(\bar B+G)$ go to the pool: it is the expected-cost margin that nets to zero against the failures over a period (§7.7), the failures' cover. The pool is **a lock on $\Omega$'s balance**, raised as each fee arrives after the debt and released at the close, so $\Omega$'s *unlocked* balance excludes it — **except that a §4.3 reviewer advance may draw on it during the period** *(2.3.0 §2.6, run 15's ruling: verification before the state's blame)*, the pool at the close being net of advances paid; inspection waits neither on the state's cash nor on the customers' rebate settlement: summonses are funded from $\Omega$'s unlocked balance — retained from earlier periods, and the capital that came back — then by the bank's loans; the pool is touched by nothing during the period. *The close.* At the period boundary — when the bank publishes the index, inside the tick before the summons pass — **the claims recorded at stops inside the period are paid from the pool, pro rata**: each claim times $\min(1,\ \text{pool}/\text{claims})$, paid to its requester as the rebate. A claim recorded earlier in the closing tick belongs to the closing period; one recorded later in it to the next. **What the pool does not cover is forgone**: not owed, not carried, not created — recorded on the request (claimed, paid, forgone) and in the period's readings. What the claims do not take is released to $\Omega$'s unlocked balance: ELEF's retained income for the period. A request whose clock is still running at the close is settled at the close of the period in which it stops. *Nothing else:* no liability stock, no order of liabilities, no payment at the stop. **Consequence — ELEF earns less, down to nothing.** In a quiet market claims are a few credits a period against fees of several hundred: paid in full, the rest retained. Under a crowd the pool is a fifth or a sixth of the claims: every late requester gets that fraction, the state retains nothing for the period, and the rest of the clock's bill is forgone — in public, on every request. **Consequence — the order matters.** Debt before the pool: a state losing on its deliveries repays the bank before it refunds its customers, so the customers of a state at the wall get less — the bank's money is everyone's. *(2.1.0 — superseded: a rebate paid at the stop from $\Omega$'s balance, else accrued as a liability, 1.6.0's stock, paid from income after the debt; run 14's flood left 27,000 of it unpaid against 7,900 of fees — Appendix O.)*

**Rule (2.1.0) — the wall.** The bank lends for a **delivery** only while the state's debt is within $\Delta_{\max}$: **one period of $\Omega$'s fee income** — the base fees and the troops' review fees of the last closed period, so the wall rises with the market's activity and with its prices; unbounded in the first period, which is the state's (as 1.6.0's first panels were). A delivery the bank will not lend for is funded from $\Omega$'s unlocked balance or not at all: it is **refused for credit** — recorded like the capacity case (the request's wait, the clock running, marked and told, §12.3), and the state retracts until its income has brought the debt back within the wall. **A review seat is lent for regardless of the wall.** The wall answers the state's losses on its *own* deliveries; an inspection seat serves a member's delivery, not the state's, and 1.6.0 found that the seat, not the treasury, is what caps the market — so inspection is never hostage to the state's delivery losses, as plants are not (§7.6), and its capital is small and returns at settlement (its loss is an overturned troop verdict's bond). *Seats refused for credit* reads zero by construction and is kept as a check. **Unbounded is the control** (2.0.0's state). The §8 hypothesis of the revision says the wall never binds under the expected cost where the state's deliveries fail at the prior; if it binds, the burn rule is wrong — the prior, the window or the formula — not the wall. **The order of $\Omega$'s payments, in one line.** An inflow to $\Omega$: debt → the period's pool (a fee) or $\Omega$'s unlocked balance (a return of capital; §4.3's reviewer advances and claims are paid from it) → at the close, the rebate claims pro rata → $\Omega$'s balance (2.2.0; 2.1.0: debt → liabilities, oldest first → $\Omega$'s balance). A summons: $\Omega$'s balance → the bank's loan — for a seat always, for a delivery while the debt is within the wall → refused for credit.

**Retired as rules by 2.1.0:** $\Omega$'s **creation** and **retirement** (2.0.0) — a loan is creation tied to a summons and repaid by it; a repayment is retirement tied to a case; "deficit outstanding" becomes three readings: **loans outstanding** (capital in flight), **debt** (losses not yet repaid), **liabilities** (rebates owed — retired by 2.2.0: a claim is paid at the close or forgone, and the readings are *claims, paid, forgone, the forgone share*). **$\Omega$'s net position** $=\Omega_U-\text{debt}$ (2.2.0; $-\text{liabilities}$ under 2.1.0). **Consequence — what a riot costs, by line.** The crowd of thirty under $N_T=40$ puts the field's capital on the loans line — about $26\times(c^D_T+G)$ at the risen prices — repaid as the cohort vests; its failed deliveries put their losses on the debt line, repaid from the riot's own fees (a riot is many awards, so the wall is high in a riot); its lateness puts rebates on the liabilities line, paid after. Each is read apart; none is printed for.

**Rule (2.0.0; superseded by 2.1.0 — kept as the control, `Params.bank` off) — the state is never broke: creation, retirement, the deficit.** $\Omega$ receives its income as before — the base fee $\omega_0 B$, the troops' review fees, the troops' prices and released locks as they come home — and pays as before: plants, rebates at the clock's stop, troop costs and locks at summons. **When $\Omega$'s unlocked balance cannot meet a payment the rules require, $\Omega$ creates the shortfall** (`creation`, a source, §4.4) and pays at once. Creation is **only** for a required payment — a summons the rules call for, a rebate the clock owes, a plant the dial calls for — never a discretionary spend (I28; the one exception is the declared deficit-shock scenario operation of a run, recognised by its note). **Retirement, in one sentence:** *while the deficit stands, every credit that reaches $\Omega$'s unlocked balance is burned against it at once* (`retirement`, a sink) — $\Omega$'s unlocked balance is zero for as long as it owes created credits, and it creates again for the next required payment; the engine runs the burn within the tick. **Deficit outstanding** $=$ created $-$ retired is $\Omega$'s negative position; **$\Omega$'s net position** $=\Omega_U-$ deficit replaces "$\Omega$'s minimum balance" as the solvency reading (§18.1 question 16); both are public, with the deficit's **peak** and **age**. **Retired as rules by 2.0.0:** the advance cap $A_T$ (1.4.0); the demand reserve $R_T$ (1.5.0) and its two-period window (1.6.0); the plant reserve and $\Omega$'s order of payment (1.6.0); the troop account as a wallet and its reserve (1.7.0) — the troops' wallet is $\Omega$ itself; rebate **liabilities** as an unpaid stock (1.6.0) — a rebate is paid when the clock stops, $\Omega$ creating if short. *Plants unfunded for want of $\Omega$* reads zero by construction and retires with the plant reserve. **Standing (under 2.1.0 too):** the duty fund (the raised tax in, uplift out); the service clock and the rebate; troops, their per-request summons, inspection first, and the delivery check for inspection *seats* in capacity terms (§12.3; the funds clause is the bank's loan since 2.1.0). **Consequence — what a riot's deficit is:** the in-flight capital of the state's share — at each summons $\Omega$ burns the troop's cost and locks its guarantee and its seats' costs and bonds, about $1.5\,\bar B$ to $2\,\bar B$ per delivery, and recovers the price and the locks at vesting, thirteen ticks later; a crowd that doubles the field creates thousands in a window and retires them as the cohort vests. The cost of a riot falls where a deficit's cost falls in any closed economy: on the price level, and so on everyone holding credits, in proportion, while the state's capital is in the field, handed back as it comes home. **Guard.** A state that can create has every reason to prefer its own troops; the guards of 1.4.0 and 1.7.0 stand — members first, the windows, the per-request summons, the state's share and income by source on the record — and delivery at cost (§7.7) removes the motive. The rules below are kept as the fixed-price controls and for the record.

**Rule (1.6.0–1.7.1; retired 2.0.0) — $\Omega$ backs the fund above a plant reserve; $\Omega$'s order of payment.** The advance cap $A_T$ of §12.3 is **removed**: when the troop account (1.7.0; the merged duty fund under 1.4.0–1.6.0) cannot carry a summons, $\Omega$ advances the difference from its unlocked balance **above a plant reserve** — the capital the honesty clock will need: the period's plant rate so far ($\pi_t\times$ the initial-review rate, floored at the previous period's) $\times(\varphi^E+\varphi^F)\times$ a horizon of ticks (Appendix A). Without the reserve the stress that summons the troops would spend the capital of the plants that detect it. Advances are repaid from the troops' wallet's inflows first — before any uplift under the merged fund, before the surplus flows to $\Omega$ since 1.7.0. **$\Omega$'s order each tick:** (1) the plant reserve is held back; (2) rebate liabilities older than one period are paid — a debt is owed, an advance is optional, and a debt does not wait more than a period; (3) advances to the fund for summonses, inspection seats before deliveries; (4) younger rebate liabilities; (5) whatever $\Omega$ keeps. Advances rank above young liabilities because scaling is what stops lateness accruing; the age bound keeps that reason from becoming an excuse. **Readings:** $\Omega$'s unlocked balance and its minimum over the period — **ELEF's solvency under stress**; the plant reserve; advance outstanding; plants unfunded for want of $\Omega$, which must read zero in every run; summonses refused for want of $\Omega$'s money; liabilities by age.

**Rule (1.7.0–1.7.1; retired 2.0.0) — $\Omega$ pays its debts from its income.** The order above is unchanged and now funded: the troop surplus of §12.3 lands in $\Omega$ at each settlement batch, before the order runs. The plant reserve's horizon is **a plant's life**, $W+T_{\text{review}}=15$ ticks — $\pi_t\times$ the initial-review rate $\times(\varphi^E+\varphi^F)\times15$, about 100 at the defaults (the implementer's 1.6.0 reading, ruled by this revision; the ticks remaining in the period is kept as a declared cell). **Consequence:** the state's lateness is paid from the state's deliveries, so paying a rebate no longer costs the troop that would have prevented the next one; plants unfunded for want of $\Omega$ must read zero, and if they do not, the state's income — not its order of payment — is the finding, and $\omega_0$ the lever (Appendix A). **Reading — $\Omega$'s record by source,** per period: its two incomes (the base fee $\omega_0 B$ per award, the troop surplus received); its own money's flows (advances made, repaid — not income); its outflows (rebates paid, liabilities serviced, plant capital out and swept back), so that questions 16 and 17 read from one place.

Per root request: in $=B+\varphi^E+\varphi^F+\omega$; out $=B$ at vesting, reimbursements from $\varphi^E$ at completion (for the root and its nested cases), fixed fees from $\varphi^F$ and emission at each settlement in the tree, the tree's expense debts from the fee pot's remainder and both pots' unused remainders at tree closure, $\omega$ to $\Omega$. Forfeited guarantees go to the requester; forfeited bonds, net of court-fee reimbursement, are burned — they do not fund fees; kill fees and court fees transfer; emission is minted into case pools. **Two accounts (1.7.0–1.7.1; one merged duty fund under 1.4.0–1.6.0; since 2.0.0 the uplift fund alone — the troops' wallet is $\Omega$, §12.3).** *The duty fund* is the uplift fund of 1.3.0 again: in, $(m_t-1)\,\omega_0 B$ per award and nothing else; out, its whole balance as uplift to the reviews settling in each batch by role weight, carrying forward what no review claims; no reserve, no lock. *The troop account* is the troops' wallet (§12.3): in, $\Omega$'s advances and every troop's earnings (fee, price, court fee won, released bond or guarantee); out, troop costs purchased and bonds or guarantees posted at summons, a guarantee forfeited at a final $\mathsf N$, a bond burned on an overturn, the repayment of any advance, and at each settlement batch the unlocked balance above the troops' reserve $R_T$ to $\Omega$. Both are wallets, so I1 holds. A plant's stood review settles in the same batch as any other and draws uplift like any other; a troop's draws none.

---

## 11. Reliability, duty credit, and the plant record

### 11.1 Reliability and duty credit

**Rule.** Two counters, never one.

- **Reliability $\rho_i$** — were your verdicts and deliveries right. Moves only on settled outcomes: a verdict or delivery that stood → up; overturned, failed, missed, or withdrawn → down. Input to $S_\theta$ and to audit rates. A human correction after settlement records a failed outcome for every contradicted assessment (§15).
- **Duty credit $\kappa_i$** — did you do verification that stood. $+1$ per review that settled standing. Never an input to $S_\theta$.

**Rule.** Nothing accrues at completion. Fee, reliability and duty credit all vest on the same event — the verdict stood at settlement. Society benefits from correct verification, not from verification events.

**Rule.** Verification is a public good and under-provision is a real risk, so duty credit is rewarded — as *privilege*, not as evidence of competence: it breaks ties in selection, reduces conscription, and releases first from duty.

**Default.** $\rho_i=(s_i+1)/(s_i+o_i+2)$ over the trailing 100 settled outcomes; $\kappa_i$ counts reviews settled in the trailing 100 ticks. A plant outcome counts in $\rho$ as any stood or overturned outcome does.

### 11.2 The plant record — negligence measured where it cannot be hidden *(1.2.0)*

**Rule.** Every actor carries a **plant record**: over its trailing $n_P$ bad plants and trailing $n_P$ good plants, its smoothed **bad-plant approval rate** and **good-plant rejection rate**,

$$\hat a_i=\frac{o^{\text{bad}}_i+1}{n^{\text{bad}}_i+2},\qquad \hat r_i=\frac{o^{\text{good}}_i+1}{n^{\text{good}}_i+2},$$

where $o^{\text{bad}}_i$ is the number of bad plants actor $i$ approved and $o^{\text{good}}_i$ the number of good plants it rejected. These are the individual counterparts of the clock $\hat F_t$ — the same quantity the dial measures for the reserve, measured per actor. A record is *undetermined* until the actor has judged $n_{\min}$ plants of the kind in question.

**Rule — two tiers of eligibility.**
- **Panel eligibility** (appeal, dispute and audit reviews): $\hat a_i\le\theta_{\text{panel}}$ and $\hat r_i\le\theta_{\text{panel}}$. An actor whose record exceeds either may still take initial reviews but may not sit in judgment on a challenge.
- **Duty eligibility** (any review): $\hat a_i\le\theta_{\text{duty}}$ and $\hat r_i\le\theta_{\text{duty}}$. An actor whose record exceeds either is **suspended** for $T_{\text{susp}}$ ticks — since 1.3.0 **from the market**, not from duty alone: it wins no new award (§5.2), finishes what it holds, and its $\rho$ carries the plant failures into selection for when it returns. **Its plant record persists.**
- An actor with an undetermined record is eligible for both tiers.

**Rule (1.3.0) — probation, the way back.** After $T_{\text{susp}}$ ticks a suspended actor becomes a **probationer**. A probationer serves **initial reviews only** — no appeal, dispute or audit seat; is assigned as any duty actor is (§7.5) and receives plants at the ordinary rate, mixed with real work it cannot tell apart; has its **audit odds pinned at 1** (§8.3); is **re-checked at every plant adjudication** — any plant failure suspends it again at once, the clock restarting; and is **readmitted to membership** — new awards, and duty as a member — when its **trailing record** (the one record above, which persists through suspension) passes $\theta_{\text{duty}}$ and at least $n_{\min}$ new plant outcomes have been adjudicated since the suspension began. One record, one meaning: the count of new outcomes is only a minimum before readmission can be considered. Panel seats return separately, when the same trailing record passes $\theta_{\text{panel}}$. On readmission the audit odds restart at $p_{\text{start}}$.

**Consequence.** A full stamper at $\hat a=0.83$ must reject about four bad plants in a row — $(4+1)/(8+2)=0.5$, some fifty honest reviews, roughly 250 ticks on duty — to work again; its panel seats return later still, once its four approvals have rolled out of the trailing window of twenty bad plants. A probationer that stamps is out again at its first bad plant, having had every approval in between audited. A probationer's own plant failure is any failure, not a failing record — intended: a careful probationer at $\varepsilon=0.05$ restarts its clock with probability about 19% over its four needed bad plants, and the alternative would let a half-stamper coast through probation on its good plants. Probation is not itself the deterrent: with every approval audited a stamper on probation still nets about $+1.2$ a review at $m=2$; what bites is the award it cannot win. *(History: 1.2.0 suspended from duty alone and re-checked a still-failing record at the next plant — the implementer's reading, confirmed by patch 1.2.1; run 4 showed suspension from duty to be a reward, Appendix H.)*

**Rule — when eligibility is checked.** Eligibility is a property at **assignment and at rotation**. A record moves whenever one of the actor's plants is adjudicated, so an actor holding an assignment may cross a threshold mid-review: it completes its active review, whose verdict is adjudicated as usual, and **leaves duty when that review completes, whether or not a replacement is available** — this overrides §12.1's "the outgoing actor stays until replaced", which would otherwise keep the negligent to make the number.

**Consequence.** Exclusion from duty no longer frees an actor to earn (1.3.0): an actor that will not inspect honestly is not a member of the market until it does; the duty that was a burden the honest carried for the negligent is the gate the negligent must pass to work again. This is also the panel-composition rule run 3 asked for, and it is safe where a $\rho$ floor was not: the plant record is adjudicated by truth, not by the panel, so a captured majority cannot protect its members' records. A full stamper approves every bad plant and reaches $\hat a=(4+1)/(4+2)=0.83>\theta_{\text{duty}}$ on its fourth bad plant — out of the reserve. A reviewer that approves half the bad plants sits near $\hat a\approx0.5$: out of every panel, and on the edge of duty. A careful reviewer at 95% accuracy sits near $\hat a\approx0.09$ once its window fills; early on, one careful reviewer in five has a single failure in its first four bad plants ($\hat a=0.33$) and is kept off panels — not off duty — until the window grows; with a full window of 20 it is off panels about 2% of the time at $\varepsilon=0.05$ and about 13% at $\varepsilon=0.10$. That price is paid in panel seats, not in money. The good-plant record $\hat r$ forms slowly — at $\beta_P=0.75$, four good plants take about 160 reviews — which is acceptable because appeals already expose false rejecters at $a\approx0.98$; $\hat r$ is the backstop for a market where actors do not appeal.

**Consequence — the bond asymmetry.** The two error directions had exposures of about 0.98 (false rejections, via appeals) and about 0.027 (false approvals, via audits) under 1.1.0. Plants expose both at the rates $\pi_t\beta_P$ and $\pi_t(1-\beta_P)$, by construction, without an appeal or a dispute. The bond stays one bond; the asymmetry is addressed through exposure, which run 3's solvency sweep showed is the only affordable route.

---

## 12. The verification reserve, rotation, and the effort dial

### 12.1 The reserve and rotation

**Rule (1.6.0) — seat the queue.** At each seating the reserve target is **at least the number of reviews waiting for a reviewer** plus the period's initial-review rate, and never less than the §12.2 sizing $X_t$; the maximum is taken at every rotation. Reviews waiting are counted by kind — root submissions without an open initial review; rolled or vacated audits without an open audit and challenge seats not yet filled (panel seats) — and the member seats are at least the panel seats waiting, within the cap. Seats are filled in the 1.3.0 order: members for member-only seats to the cap and the floor, probationers lowest duty credit first for initial seats — **all of them if the queue needs them** — members for remaining initial demand to the cap; a review still unfilled after $w_R$ is a troop's (§12.3). The eligibility shortfall counts what no eligible free actor could fill before the troop did. **The members' duty cap** of the 1.3.0 rule below keeps its rule; 1.6.0 swept its default $\lceil E_t/2\rceil$ against $\lceil 2E_t/3\rceil$ and $E_t$, and 1.7.0 moved it to $\lceil 2E_t/3\rceil$ on run 8's cap table (Appendix L) — more inspection at every moment, a third of the members free to deliver where the floor does not bind; $\lceil E_t/2\rceil$ is the 1.6.0 control.

**Rule.** The reserve holds $\min(X_t,\ \text{eligible})$ actors on verification duty — idle on duty or reviewing — outside ordinary commitments, where $X_t$ is the reserve size the dial sets each period (§12.2) with a floor of $V_{\max}+1$, and $V_{\max}$ is the maximum **review positions** one case can hold on a challenge path: initial plus two appeal, or audit plus one — three. Since 1.3.0 the reserve has **two kinds of seat**: the **member seats** — the period's expected audit and challenge reviews, which only members may hold — and the **initial seats** for the rest of the demand, which probationers fill first. A withdrawn position is vacated and refilled; distinct actors over a case's lifetime are unbounded under replacement. Four counts are kept: reserve membership, *occupancy* (eligible duty actors are busy), *solvency shortfall* (fewer than $V_{\max}$ duty actors can fund a bond), and since 1.2.0 the *eligibility shortfall*: when fewer actors are eligible by plant record (§11.2) than $X_t$ calls for, the reserve holds the eligible ones, the gap is recorded, and reviews wait as §7.5 says. **The loop does not seat a negligent reviewer to make a number.** A market with two honest reviewers in twenty lacks reviewers, and the record says so.

**Rule (1.3.0; the fraction moved 1.7.0) — who carries the load.** **No member is seated on duty while on-duty members number $\lceil 2E_t/3\rceil$ or more** ($\lceil E_t/2\rceil$ under 1.3.0–1.6.0), where $E_t$ is the number of actors eligible for duty and not suspended; a member found above the cap when $E_t$ falls completes its active review and leaves duty at its completion. Probationers on duty do not count against the cap. **The panel floor yields over the cap, bounded by availability:** at least $\min(V_{\max}-1,\ E_t)$ members are on duty whenever that many free, fundable members exist to seat; a reserve below the floor is admissible only when none was left — a member committed to a task, unable to fund the bond, or mid-review elsewhere cannot be seated — and the gap counts toward the eligibility shortfall. The reserve is filled in this order: **members first for the member seats**, never fewer than the floor; **then probationers for the initial seats**, lowest duty credit first — an idle member holding an initial seat makes room for a free probationer; **then members** for any initial demand left, up to the cap. Any remaining gap is the eligibility shortfall, and reviews wait. Initial reviews are assigned round-robin among all on-duty actors, probationers included; audit, appeal and dispute seats only to on-duty members who are panel-eligible. **The members' cap yields to the reserve floor $V_{\max}+1$ as the panel floor yields to the cap:** a reserve that cannot seat $V_{\max}+1$ fresh reviewers cannot run the loop, so at memberships of four to six the floor wins (four to seven under the half cap); at larger memberships the two agree. *(The implementer's reading, confirmed as the rule by patch 1.3.1, October 17, 2026.)*

**Rule.** Duty rotates: replacement before release. A turn ends after a fixed number of reviews that stood or a fixed number of ticks; the incoming replacement is the free, eligible actor with the lowest duty credit, among those who were **off duty when the tick's rotation began** — so a released actor has at least one tick of ordinary eligibility. A seat a turn vacates is refilled **for the kind of seat it was**: a member seat by a member, an initial seat by a probationer first — a member holding an initial seat beyond the member seats is replaced by a probationer when one is free. **An outgoing probationer whose turn ends with no free probationer stays**, rather than being replaced by a member the next tick's "probationers first" would hand back *(the implementer's reading, confirmed as the rule by patch 1.3.1)*. If no replacement is available the outgoing actor stays — unless it is duty-ineligible, in which case it leaves when its active review completes, replacement or not (§11.2). After the turns, the reserve is filled in the order above or shrunk to $X_t$, idle probationers first, then idle members above the member seats and the floor. Volunteers may stay beyond a turn.

**Default.** $X_0=4$ (the initial reserve; from the first period $X_t$ is sized by §12.2); a turn is 3 reviews or 30 ticks.

### 12.2 The effort dial and the alarm *(1.2.0)*

**Rule.** The orchestrator maintains a global **effort multiplier** $m_t\ge1$, public, updated once per period from the clock of §7.6:

$$m_{t+1}=\operatorname{clamp}\!\Big(m_t\cdot\big(\hat F_t/F^*\big)^{\gamma},\ \ 0.9\,m_t,\ \ 2\,m_t\Big)\ \ \text{and then into}\ [1,\ m_{\max}],$$

where $\hat F_t$ is the bad-plant approval rate measured over the period, $F^*$ the target rate, $\gamma$ the gain. In words: when measured negligence exceeds the target, effort rises — at most doubling per period; when it is below, effort eases — at most 10% per period. Up fast, down slow, because capture is expensive and recovery is hard; bounded steps, because the dial must be predictable and not oscillate. A period with no bad plant adjudicated gives no reading, and $m_t$ holds. This is the block-difficulty adjustment with negligence in place of block time and planted work in place of timestamps.

**Rule — what the dial moves.** Four things scale with $m_t$, together:

1. **The plant rate**: $\pi_t=\pi_0\,m_t$ (capped at $\pi_{\max}$).
2. **Audit odds**: each reviewer's adaptive $p_j$ is applied as $\min(1,\ m_t\,p_j)$ (§8.3).
3. **The reserve**: $X_t$ is sized to the expected review demand of the period, with a floor of $V_{\max}+1$ (2.8.0: the period's approvals and the audit demand count a plant's pending $\mathsf F$ as a real root's, or $X_t$ and the members' seats are undersized by $\pi_t$ of the approvals). *Default sizing:* $X_t=\max\big(V_{\max}+1,\ \lceil r_t\,\bar d\,\rceil\big)$, where $r_t$ is the expected number of reviews per tick — the period's real initial reviews per tick times $(1+\pi_t)$, plus the **audit demand on applied odds** (1.3.0): the sum over the period's initial approvals of the approving reviewer's applied odds, $1$ for a probationer or an undetermined newcomer and $m_t p_j$ otherwise, per tick; plus the period's rate of challenge reviews — and $\bar d$ the mean review duration in ticks, at least 1. The **member seats** (§12.1) are the audit demand plus the challenge rate, times $\bar d$, floored at $\min(V_{\max}-1,E_t)$.
4. **The service fee**: $\omega_t=\omega_0\,m_t$ (§4.1). Verification effort is funded by the economy's security tax, and the tax rises with the threat. $\Omega$ funds plants from it.

**Rule — the alarm.** If $m_t=m_{\max}$ and $\hat F_t>F^*$ for two consecutive periods, the orchestrator commits a **capture alarm** to the History and re-seats the duty reserve from the actors with the lowest $\hat a_i$ (ties by lowest $\hat r_i$, then duty credit) — the one place a record may choose the panel, because this record cannot be captured. Duty actors holding a review leave at its completion if their record then fails; the rest are released and the seats refilled. The alarm is also the signal to a human, if any is listening; the loop does not wait for one.

**Rule — working capital.** A plant ties up its two pots — $\varphi^E+\varphi^F$, 15 credits at the defaults (1.3.0; 45 under 1.2.0, of which 30 was $\Omega$ paying itself, §7.6) — from creation until its tree closes. Until 2.1.0 the plant count the dial calls for in a period was **capped by what $\Omega$ can fund from its unlocked balance**; a plant $\Omega$ cannot fund is refused whole, and the gap at the period's end is recorded as a **plant shortfall**. **Since 2.1.0 the bank mints the pots and no plant waits for $\Omega$'s cash** (§7.6): inspection's budget is not the state's purse — 1.6.0's plant reserve, now by construction and without the reserve; *plants posted against plants called for* is the reading. $\Omega$'s cold-start float carries one period of plants at $m=1$ under the 1.2.0 count; under 1.3.0 it carries three times the plants on the same float (Appendix A).

**Rule (1.3.0; restored exactly by 1.7.0) — the raised tax pays the reviewers.** Of the service fee $\omega_t=\omega_0 m_t B$ collected on each award, $\Omega$ keeps the base $\omega_0 B$ and the remainder $(m_t-1)\,\omega_0 B$ goes to the **duty fund**. At each settlement batch the fund's balance is distributed to the reviews settling in that batch **exactly as emission is** — by role weight, across every case settling that tick — as an **uplift** on the fixed fee. Unlike emission the fund is real money paid in, so a balance no review claims in a tick carries to the next. Under 1.4.0–1.6.0 the fund was the troops' wallet too and paid uplift only from above the troops' reserve, so the troops' surplus reached the reviewers; since 1.7.0 the fund receives the raised tax and nothing else and pays its whole balance — the reviewers' uplift is the dial's tax and not a share of work they did not do (§12.3). When the dial raises effort, the tax it levies is paid to the actors supplying the effort, not banked by the referee: at $m=2$ and $B=20$, one extra credit per task — about 3 a tick at 3 tasks a tick — shared among the roughly four weight-units settling each tick is an uplift of about 0.75 per unit of role weight on a fee of 2; at $m_{\max}=4$, about 2.3.

### 12.3 Troops: summons, the fund's order, minimization *(1.4.0)*

**Rule — summons for a seat.** A review seat the rules require — an initial review, an audit, an appeal review, a dispute panel seat — that has gone **unfilled for $w_R$ ticks** is taken by a troop, when the fund can carry it (2.0.0: when $N_T$ has a place for it — $\Omega$ creates what a summons needs; 2.1.0: on $\Omega$'s balance, else the bank's loan, **a seat regardless of the wall**, §10.4). A seat is unfilled at a tick when, after that tick's seating of the reserve (§12.1), no on-duty reviewer eligible for that seat is free to take it. The clock runs from the moment the seat was required: submission for an initial review, the roll for an audit, admission for an appeal, filing for a dispute; a seat has waited one at the seating of the tick it was required and $w_R$ at the seating of the tick $w_R-1$ later. A troop takes **that seat only** and is released at the review's completion — a review resumed after a resubmission is the same assignment, and the troop resumes it. **One audit no troop takes:** the audit of a troop reviewer's approval of a troop's delivery (§8.3) — the orchestrator does not audit itself — so that seat is the one that may wait past its window for a member; the History counts cases **held for the market's audit** and their age.

**Rule — the engine summons, the driver answers.** The summons is a **Rule the engine applies** at the tick's seating step: the engine can see that a required seat has waited $w_R$ ticks with no free eligible on-duty reviewer, or that root requests have waited $w_A$ beyond the free fundable members (the award rule below), and that the fund can carry the cost; it creates the troop, posts its bond or guarantee, purchases its cost and records the summons in the History. The troop's **verdict and delivery are the driver's**: a declared disposition — the truth with probability $1-\varepsilon_T$, delivery failing at $b$ — supplied through the ordinary review and submission operations on the troop's seat. The engine has no roll, no selection and no truth; the driver has all three; this keeps that division. A run that answers for a troop otherwise than it declares produces an implementation effect, declared under §18.2.

**Rule — panels.** A troop may hold an appeal or dispute seat, so a challenge may be decided by a member and a troop, or by two troops; a split between a member and a troop goes to the orchestrator's ruling as any split does. A troop never sits on a plant's panel — until 2.8.0 plants carried no challenge; since 2.8.0 a plant's panel seat past $w_R$ is the orchestrator's own verdict (§7.6). No troop is summoned for a case a human has ruled on.

**Rule — the dial per seat (2.9.0).** The dial is read per planted wrong step: one multiplier each — $m^A_t$ on $\hat F^A_t$ (the share of planted approvals confirmed at the audit seat in the period among those adjudicated there), $m^{La}_t$ and $m^{Ld}_t$ on the share of planted lying appeals and disputes a panelist sided with — stepping as $m_t$ steps (within $[0.9, 2]$ a period, $1 \le m \le m_{\max}$), each against the one target $F^*$ and each moving **its rate alone** ($\pi^A_t = \pi^A_0 m^A_t$; $\pi^{La}_t$, $\pi^{Ld}_t$ likewise), not the odds, $X_t$ or $\omega$, which answer $m_t$ as before: the audit stamper's price is its record, not its odds. A multiplier whose reading is undefined in a period does not move. The planted lies' quotas for a period are the trailing closed period's real appeals and disputes times their rates, with a floor of two of each kind. The plant rate $\pi_t$ and its multiplier $m_t$ on the initial adjudications are unchanged; the planted verdict is not an initial adjudication and never reaches the dial's clock. The period's approvals and the audit demand count a planted approval's F at odds 1; its waiting initial seat counts as a waiting case's. **Since the ruling after run 38 (Appendix V):** each per-seat multiplier steps on a reading of at least ten adjudications of its kind, the periods' counts pooled forward until reached (the reading taken resets the pool; the lie dial's ×2 on one error at four seats was the reading), and within its own bound — $m^A_{\max}$ 20 and $m^{L}_{\max}$ 8 (Appendix A), so $\pi^A_{\max}$ and $\pi^L_{\max}$ are reachable; the dial's own $m_{\max}$ 4 is unchanged.

**Rule — summons for an award.** A root request that **no eligible member has been awarded within $w_A$ ticks of its posting** — because no member free to commit and able to fund the guarantee offered — is awarded to a troop, when the fund can carry it, **at the trailing market price** $\bar B$: the mean price awarded to members over the trailing period, the ceiling $\beta$ when there is none (2.0.0: the price level $P_t$ when there is none — $P_0$ before the first award — since the ceiling is the requester's choice now; and *members' awards only* is the rule, so that a state delivering most of the market cannot set its own price). **Read per request, not per tick** *(ruled after run 9; patch 1.7.1, October 18, 2026)*: a free member takes the oldest request it can; the others, unawarded past their window with nobody offering for them, are the state's. So at the seating step the requests that have waited $w_A$ and **exceed the free fundable members** — members off duty, uncommitted, holding no review, able to fund $G$ at the market price — are summoned for, **oldest first, after the members have taken theirs**: the oldest of them, as many as there are free fundable members, are left to the market, and the rest are the state's at that seating. One free member does not hold the state back from the rest of the queue. *(1.4.0–1.7.0's engine read the rule per tick — any one free fundable member blocked every award summons that tick, however long the queue; run 9 traced the 50/50 collapse to it, Appendix L.)* A troop is summoned only for a request whose ceiling admits **the state's price** $p_T=\max(\bar B,\underline{B})$ (2.4.0; $\beta\ge\bar B$ before it): a request priced below the market is not a shortage and waits; a request priced below the state's reservation is marked and told, and the state takes it at no wait (§5.1). **The award pass gathers only what the state may take** (2.4.0, a conformance reading of 1.7.1 made at acceptance, Appendix Q): the requests counted against the free fundable members are those at $\beta\ge p_T$, so a request below the state's price neither holds a member's slot it will not use nor pushes a fundable request beyond the count. **The troop delivers at $p_T$** and the requester pays in exactly as for any award ($p_T+\varphi^E+\varphi^F+\omega_t$, from the posting lock, which covers it since the floor only raises the state's price toward $\beta$); the troop's guarantee $G=\lceil 0.5\,p_T\rceil$ is locked from the fund (2.4.0: at the state's price; $\bar B$ before it), the state's expected cost is burned at $p_T$ — $c^D_T=\max(0,(1-b_T)\,p_T-b_T G)$, §7.7, neutral in expectation at the floor as at $\bar B$ — and $P_t$ counts the award at $p_T$; the troop delivers once, **within the ordinary commitment**, and its submission is reviewed as any submission — by the on-duty reserve, round-robin; by a troop reviewer only under the seat rule above. The troop is released at the case's vesting or final settlement. **Members first, always:** a troop never takes an award a member offered for, never takes a seat a member or probationer is free to fill, and is never preferred to one on price, reputation or duty credit; the window is the whole of the market's chance, and nothing shortens it. *(Until 2.0.0 every offer was at the ceiling, so $\bar B=\beta$ and "$\beta\ge\bar B$" never bound; since 2.0.0 it binds where the requesters post below the market, and since 2.4.0 the floor binds where the members' price is below the anchor cost — the patient cells of run 17 and the edge of its faster-decay cell; every quiet cell of runs 12–17 has $\bar B\ge 20$ and never meets it.)* **Consequence (2.4.0) — what the floor does and does not do.** It does not let the state set the price: above $\underline{B}$ the state follows the members as before. It stops the state from being driven below the market's anchor cost by a demand side that waits: at the floor the state declines, and the price can fall further only where members cheaper than the anchor choose to bid — which is a market, not a spiral.

**Rule (2.0.0) — the capacity case; two places; the troops' wallet.** The state fields at most **$N_T$** troops at once, deliveries and review seats together (default 40, Appendix A: clear of the quiet field at every split in run 10 — 18 / 23 / 26 / 29 at 50–80%, peaks to 34 — and inside every riot peak of run 11, 75–221; unbounded is the control). **A delivery's summons needs room in the field for itself and its inspection seat — two of $N_T$** — else the field would fill with deliveries whose inspections are then refused: 1.6.0's "a delivery carries its inspection", in capacity terms. **Ruled at acceptance (Appendix M): the two places are *reserved, and released on seating*** — a troop delivery holds two places from its summons until its initial review is assigned: to a market reviewer, after which it holds one; to a troop, after which the seat holds its own; the capacity reading counts places held. A summons $N_T$ refuses is **the capacity case** — the one case the market cannot answer with a price: the request waits, its clock runs, and the case is recorded — *refused for capacity*, the request's wait in seating steps, and that its requester was told (2.0.0: a flag at the first refusal; **2.1.0 — every refusal is told:** a summons $N_T$ refuses, and a delivery the bank refuses for credit (§10.4), **mark the request** — *refused for capacity* / *refused for credit*, with the tick — and the mark is readable by the requester's policy through the public view, as the below-market flag is; the requester may hold or withdraw (withdrawn before award, owed nothing), the clock runs either way, and the share of marked requests withdrawn is the busy line's reading beside on-time share and lateness — and, since 2.2.0, the **forgone share** of the period's rebate claims, the unpaid part of the state's blame, the busy line's third reading; the message itself is 2.x); the oldest-first rule of I26 applies to refusals for capacity as it did to refusals for funds. **The troops' wallet is $\Omega$** (§10.4): a troop's cost is burned from $\Omega$ and its locks sit on $\Omega$ — created if short under 2.0.0; **lent by the bank since 2.1.0, tied to the summons** — and its price and released locks come home to $\Omega$, repaying that loan first and then the order of every inflow (debt, liabilities, $\Omega$). Without $N_T$ the state is an infinite supplier at $\bar B$ and nothing is ever scarce; under it the state's share is bounded, bids rise toward ceilings when members and troops are both short, and the price level responds to money.

**Rule — the troop account *(1.7.0–1.7.1; retired 2.0.0, the troops' wallet is $\Omega$; 1.4.0–1.6.0: "the fund carries the troops first")*.** The troops' wallet is a separate **troop account** (§10.4). **In:** $\Omega$'s advances; every troop's earnings — fee, price, court fee won, released bond or guarantee. **Out:** troop costs purchased and bonds or guarantees posted at summons; a troop's guarantee forfeited to the requester at a final $\mathsf N$; a troop reviewer's bond burned on an overturn (the court fee to the winner); repayment of $\Omega$'s advances; and at each settlement batch **the unlocked balance above the reserve $R_T$ flows to $\Omega$**. $R_T$ is the two-period peak of the capital troops held (the rule below); the summons rules, inspection first, the delivery check and the plant reserve are as 1.6.0, with the troop account in the fund's place. **Withdrawn:** 1.4.0's clause *"nothing a troop earns reaches $\Omega$ except by repayment of an advance"* — a troop is an actor of the state and its earnings are the state's. **What guards the conflict of interest the clause was for** — a state that earns from troops has a reason to prefer them — all stand: members first at every seat (a troop is summoned only for a seat or award no eligible actor took within its window); the windows $w_R$, $w_A$; cost at summons; no emission, no uplift, no $\rho$, no $\kappa$ to a troop; the state's share on the public record — troop-ticks, troop deliveries, panels held by troops, per period; and, added by 1.7.0, **$\Omega$'s income by source** (§10.4), so that a state living off its own deliveries is visible as such. **Consequence:** the reviewers' uplift is what 1.3.0 promised them; the state's deliveries pay the state — at 60/40 in run 8 the troops' net was 6 to 7 a tick, the deliveries' price $\bar B$ less $c^D_T=10$ with the guarantee back at vesting and forfeited at a final $\mathsf N$, and the review seats' fees (a troop reviewer's cost is reimbursed from the requester's allowance like any reviewer's, §7.3, so an audit seat nets its fee of 3 — the accepted text's "nets nothing" is corrected in Appendix L); the honest end poorer by the troops' former share of their uplift, about two fifths of it at 60/40, and still far richer than the stampers.

**Under 1.4.0–1.6.0 (superseded).** The duty fund was the troops' wallet and kept its name. **In:** the raised tax $(m_t-1)\,\omega_0 B$ per award, as before; every troop's earnings. **Out, in this order each tick:** (1) troop costs at summons and bonds or guarantees posted; (2) repayment of any advance from $\Omega$; (3) at each settlement batch, the **uplift** of §12.2 — distributed exactly as before, but only from the fund's **unlocked balance above a reserve** $R_T$ — since 1.5.0 the peak troop *demand* of the current period (next rule); under 1.4.0 it was the largest capital troops had tied up at once during the trailing period, which the uplift drained the fund to every batch, so that the peak could never rise (Appendix J). At rest $R_T=0$ and the whole fund flows to the reviewers as under 1.3.0; under stress the fund keeps what the period's demand required. **The advance:** when the fund cannot carry a summons, $\Omega$ advances the difference from its unlocked balance, up to $A_T$ outstanding; the advance is repaid first from the fund's inflows. Beyond $A_T$ the summons fails and is counted as **troop shortfall**. $\Omega$ keeps the base rate $\omega_0 B$ and the plant capital, as before; nothing a troop earns reaches $\Omega$ except by repayment of an advance.

**Rule — the reserve is sized to the need *(1.5.0; retired 2.0.0)*.** At each seating step $s$ the **troop demand** is
$$D_s=\sum_{\text{troops holding a seat at }s}\text{capital held}\;+\;\sum_{\text{cases whose summons the fund could not carry at }s}\text{the capital the case's unfilled seats would have drawn},$$
where *could not carry* means **refused for funds** — the carry check's own refusal, the branch that counts troop shortfall; a request priced below $\bar B$ is the rule's own exclusion, a requester that cannot pay in is the market's refusal, a challenge path already full is not a need, and none of these adds to demand. **A case's demand is its unfilled seats** — a delivery one, an audit one, a challenge missing both panel seats two, $2\,(c_T+G^V)$ — and **once per seating** is about ticks, not seats: a case refused at ten consecutive seatings adds its seats' cost to each of those ten stocks, never ten times to one; a case refused at $s$ and carried at $s+1$ appears in $D_s$ as refused cost and in $D_{s+1}$ as capital held, and no stock counts it twice. **The refused cost is the amount the carry check refused**, as it would have been purchased and posted at $s$: a delivery's $c^D_T$ plus its indexed guarantee $G=\max(1,\lceil G_{\text{frac}}\bar B\rceil)$, a seat's $c_T$ plus the case's indexed bond — not the Appendix A defaults. **The reserve is the peak demand of the period**, $R_T=\max_{s}D_s$ — under 1.5.0 over the current period alone, starting at the period's first seating from the demand then standing; under 1.6.0 over the current and the previous period (the rule below). **Since 1.7.0 the troop account's reserve is the two-period peak of the capital troops held** — costs purchased plus bonds and guarantees locked, every troop in the field — **and no longer counts refused summonses.** 1.5.0 added the refused component so a fund $\Omega$ backed only to $A_T$ could grow into the need; 1.6.0 removed $A_T$, and the component became retention without a purpose — under stress most of a 952–3,494 reserve was refused demand held back from an $\Omega$ that could not fund plants (run 8). What $R_T$ is for now, and the reason the account holds anything back at all: **the troops' in-flight capacity is shielded from the liability queue** — $\Omega$ pays liabilities older than a period before it advances (§10.4), so an account that kept nothing would see every summons compete with every old debt, and the state's share would fall exactly when the debts are largest. With peak-held capital retained the state sustains its current share from its own account as deliveries vest and the capital returns; growing that share draws on $\Omega$ after the debts, which is the right order. The surplus reaches $\Omega$ only once the account stands at $R_T$ and only while $R_T$ is not rising; in a ramp, and after every new peak in the field, $\Omega$'s income is the base fee alone. $D_s$ and its refused part remain readings (§18.1 questions 13 and 14). *A peak holds:* the stock $D_s$ falls as the backlog is served, the reserve does not — it stands at the period's peak until the period ends and comes down to the standing demand at the next boundary; the money returns to the reviewers at the period's end, not the moment the shortage passes, and that retention — one period, the dial's own lag — is a feature: a fund that has just been short does not pay out the moment it is not. *The fund grows into the need:* a refusal raises the reserve by what was refused, the fund keeps its inflows until it can carry the summonses that were made, and since every troop seat is net-positive to the fund at the defaults (a review about $+2$, a delivery about $+4$ in expectation and $+10$ realised at a vesting) a fund allowed to keep what it earns grows into the demand on its own; what bounds the state's share is then the market's unmet need and the fund's inflows, as the rule above intended.
